```
{"results":[{"title":"Billing in Azure Container Apps","content":"# Billing in Azure Container Apps\n## Consumption plan\nBilling for apps running in the Consumption plan consists of two types of charges:\n1. **Resource consumption**: The amount of resources allocated to your container app on a per-second basis, billed in vCPU-seconds and GiB-seconds.\n2. **HTTP requests**: The number of HTTP requests your container app receives.\nThe following resources are free during each calendar month, per subscription:\n1. The first 180,000 vCPU-seconds\n2. The first 360,000 GiB-seconds\n3. The first 2 million HTTP requests\nFree usage doesn't appear on your bill. You're only charged when your resource usage exceeds the monthly free grants amounts.\nNote\nIf you use Container Apps with [your own virtual network](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/custom-virtual-networks#managed-resources) or your apps utilize other Azure resources, additional charges might apply. The managed OpenTelemetry agent, when enabled, runs at no extra compute cost.\n### Resource consumption charges\nAzure Container Apps runs replicas of your application based on the [scaling rules and replica count limits](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/scale-app) you configure for each revision. [Azure Container Apps jobs](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/jobs) run replicas when job executions are triggered. You're charged for the amount of resources allocated to each replica while it's running.\nTwo meters track resource consumption:\n1. **vCPU-seconds**: The number of vCPU cores allocated to your container app on a per-second basis.\n2. **GiB-seconds**: The amount of memory allocated to your container app on a per-second basis.\n3. **GPU-seconds**: The number of GPUs allocated to your container apps on a per-second basis.\nThe first 180,000 vCPU-seconds and 360,000 GiB-seconds in each subscription per calendar month are free.\n#### Container apps\nThe rate you pay for resource consumption depends on the state of your container app's revisions and replicas. By default, replicas are charged at an *active* rate. However, in certain conditions, a replica can enter an *idle* state. While in an *idle* state, resources are billed at a reduced rate.\n##### No replicas are running\nWhen a revision is scaled to zero replicas, no resource consumption charges are incurred.\n##### Minimum number of replicas are running\nIdle usage charges might apply when a container app's revision is running under a specific set of circumstances. To be eligible for idle charges, a revision must be:","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/billing#consumption-plan"},{"title":"Heroku to Azure Container Apps migration overview","content":"# Heroku to Azure Container Apps migration overview\n## Cost comparison\n| Scenario | Heroku (Standard-1X) | Azure Container Apps (Consumption)| \n|  --- | --- | ---  |\n| Idle app (24/7) | ~ $25/month per dyno | $0 (scaled to zero) |\n| Low-traffic app | ~ $25/month per dyno | ~ $1–5/month |\n| High-traffic app (10 instances) | ~ $250/month | Varies by actual CPU and memory use |\n| Monthly free grant | None | 180,000 vCPU-seconds + 2 million requests |\n\nFor detailed pricing, see [Azure Container Apps pricing](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/details/container-apps/).\n## Workers and background jobs\nIf your Heroku app uses worker dynos (defined in a `Procfile`), deploy each worker type as a separate Container App within the same environment. Use queue-based scaling instead of a fixed instance count.\n| Heroku pattern | Azure Container Apps pattern| \n|  --- | ---  |\n| `web` process type | Container App with HTTP ingress and HTTP-based scaling |\n| `worker` process type | Container App (no ingress) with queue-based scaling |\n| Scheduled tasks (Heroku Scheduler) | [Container Apps job](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/container-apps/jobs) with a cron schedule |\n| One-off tasks (`heroku run`) | [Container Apps job](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/container-apps/jobs) with manual trigger |\n","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/migrate-heroku-overview#cost-comparison"},{"title":"Azure Spring Apps retirement announcement","content":"# Azure Spring Apps retirement announcement\n## FAQ\nCustomers using Azure Container Apps should be able to achieve most of their hosting needs for Spring applications, including managed Spring components, Java metrics, and diagnostics support. For more information, see [Java on Azure Container Apps overview](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/java-overview). If you have any questions, open a support ticket from the Azure portal or open an issue in the [Azure Container Apps](https://github.com/microsoft/azure-container-apps/issues) repository on GitHub.\n### Are there pricing differences across Microsoft solutions?\nAzure Spring Apps operates on a consumption-based model with a basic unit where you only pay for vCPU and memory for your apps.\n[Azure Container Apps](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/details/container-apps/) offers the following two pricing models:\n1. A consumption model that's billed based on per-second resource allocation - on VCPU and memory - and requests.\n2. A dedicated model with a single tenancy guarantee, access to specialized hardware, and more predictable pricing.\nBilling for the dedicated plan is based on the number of vCPU seconds and gibibyte (GiB) seconds allocated across Azure Container Apps instances. Azure Container Apps also provides a savings plan. The costs for Microsoft solutions vary based on the solution's pricing model and optimizations that can be enabled. We recommend using the [Azure Pricing Calculator](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/calculator/?ef_id=_k_8d2e1450f88b14d2046272e613f0ee0b_k_&REDACTED&msclkid=8d2e1450f88b14d2046272e613f0ee0b), which provides details on meters, usage prices, and available savings plans to accurately assess anticipated costs.\n### What is the impact for customers using Tanzu components with Azure Spring Apps Enterprise?\nIf you want to obtain or continue using Spring commercial support and Tanzu components, we recommend migrating to AKS. Work with your Broadcom sales representative to explore how to purchase and run Tanzu on AKS.\n### How can I stay up to date with Azure Spring Apps retirement guidance?\nThe following table indicates the overall release timeline for the Azure Spring Apps retirement period. We update it when new tooling guidance is ready for release.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/spring-apps/basic-standard/retirement-announcement#faq"},{"title":"Azure Container Apps express frequently asked questions (FAQs)","content":"# Azure Container Apps express frequently asked questions (FAQs)\n## Features and capabilities\n### Can I use private (virtual network) inbound or outbound networking with express?\nNo. Express apps run with a public default ingress domain and don't support VNet integration for inbound traffic, private endpoints, or VNet-routed outbound traffic. If your workload requires private networking, use Azure Container Apps with workload profiles. VNet support is on the express roadmap. For the latest status, see the [express documentation](https://aka.ms/aca/express).\n### When will express reach feature parity with Azure Container Apps?\nExpress is shipping new features on a rapid cadence throughout the preview. The [express documentation](https://aka.ms/aca/express) includes a current feature support matrix that is updated frequently.\n## Regions\n### Which Azure regions support express?\nExpress is available in the following regions during preview:\n1. East Asia\n2. West Central US\nRegion availability is expanding throughout the preview period.\n### Why can't I create an express environment in my preferred region?\nDuring preview, express is available in a specific set of regions. The portal and CLI may display regions where express isn't yet supported. If you receive an `ExpressEnvironmentNotAvailableInRegion` error, that region isn't yet supported. Region availability is expanding as the preview progresses.\n## Pricing and billing\n### How is express billed?\nExpress uses the existing Azure Container Apps consumption pricing model: per-second vCPU and memory billing with scale-to-zero. There's no environment provisioning fee. You benefit from the same free grant that applies to consumption workloads today. Express is the most cost-effective way to run web applications and APIs on Azure Container Apps. For detailed pricing, see the [Azure Container Apps pricing page](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/details/container-apps/).\n### Is there a free tier for express?\nYes. Express includes the same generous free grant that applies to Azure Container Apps consumption workloads. You aren't charged until your usage exceeds the free grant thresholds. Combined with scale-to-zero, express ensures you only pay for what you use.\n### Is there a cost for image caching or image pulls in express?\nNo. Express doesn't charge a separate fee for image caching or registry pulls. You only pay the standard Azure Container Apps consumption rates for vCPU and memory while your app is running. Any registry-side egress or storage costs are billed by your container registry, not by express.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/express-faq#can-i-use-private-virtual-network-inbound-or-outbound-networking-with-express"},{"title":"Billing in Azure Container Apps","content":"# Billing in Azure Container Apps\n## Consumption plan\n1. Configured with a [minimum replica count](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/scale-app) greater than zero\n2. Scaled to the minimum replica count\nNote\nIdle usage charges don't apply to serverless GPU apps. They're always billed for active usage.\nUsage charges are calculated individually for each replica. A replica is considered idle when *all* of the following conditions are true:\n1. The replica is running in a revision that is currently eligible for idle charges.\n2. All of the containers in the replica have started and are running.\n3. The replica isn't processing any HTTP requests.\n4. The replica is using less than 0.01 vCPU cores.\n5. The replica is receiving less than 1,000 bytes per second of network traffic.\nWhen a replica is idle, resource consumption charges are calculated at the reduced idle rates. When a replica isn't idle, the active rates apply.\n##### More than the minimum number of replicas are running\nWhen a revision is scaled above the [minimum replica count](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/scale-app), all of its running replicas are charged for resource consumption at the active rate.\n#### Jobs\nIn the Consumption plan, resources consumed by Azure Container Apps jobs are charged the active rate. Idle charges don't apply to jobs because executions stop consuming resources once the job completes.\n### Request charges\nIn addition to resource consumption, Azure Container Apps also charges based on the number of HTTP requests received by your container app. Only requests that come from outside a Container Apps environment are billable.\n1. The first 2 million requests in each subscription per calendar month are free.\n2. [Health probe](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/health-probes) requests aren't billable.\nRequest charges don't apply to Azure Container Apps jobs because they don't support ingress.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/billing#consumption-plan"},{"title":"Estimating consumption-based costs","content":"# Estimating consumption-based costs\n## Consumption-based costs\nThe way that consumption-based costs are calculated, including free grants, depends on the specific plan. For the most current cost and grant information, see the [Azure Functions pricing page](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/details/functions/).\n### **Flex Consumption plan**\nThere are two modes by which your costs are determined when running your apps in the Flex Consumption plan. Each mode is determined on a per-instance basis.\n| Billing mode | Description| \n|  --- | ---  |\n| **On Demand** | When running in *on demand* mode, you are billed only for the amount of time your function code is executing on your available instances. In on demand mode, no minimum instance count is required. You're billed for:• The total amount of memory provisioned while each on demand instance is *actively* executing functions (in GB-seconds), minus a free grant of GB-s per month.• The total number of executions, minus a free grant (number) of executions per month. |\n| **Always ready** | You can configure one or more instances, assigned to specific trigger types (HTTP/Durable/Blob) and individual functions, that are always available to handle requests. When you have any always ready instances enabled, you're billed for:• The total amount of memory provisioned across all of your always ready instances, known as the *baseline* (in GB-seconds).• The total amount of memory provisioned during the time each always ready instance is *actively* executing functions (in GB-seconds).• The total number of executions.In always ready billing, there are no free grants. |\n\nFor the most up-to-date information on execution pricing, always ready baseline costs, and free grants for on demand executions, see the [Azure Functions pricing page](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/details/functions/#pricing).\nThis diagram shows how on-demand costs are determined in this plan:\n![Graph of Flex Consumption plan on-demand costs based on both load (instance count) and time.](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-functions/media/flex-consumption-plan/billing-graph.png)\nIn addition to execution time, when you use one or more always ready instances, you pay a lower, baseline rate for the number of always ready instances you maintain. Execution time for always ready instances might be cheaper than execution time on instances with on demand execution.\nImportant\nThis article uses on-demand pricing to help you understand example calculations. Always check the current costs on the [Azure Functions pricing page](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/details/functions/) when estimating costs you might incur while running your functions in the Flex Consumption plan.\nConsider a function app that has only HTTP triggers with these basic facts:\n1. HTTP triggers handle 40 constant requests per second.\n2. HTTP triggers handle 10 concurrent requests.\n3. The instance memory size is 2,048 MB.\n4. You configure *no always ready instances*, which means the app can scale to zero.\nIn a situation like this, pricing depends more on the kind of work done during code execution. Let's look at two workload scenarios:\n1. **CPU-bound workload:** In a CPU-bound workload, there's no advantage to processing multiple requests in parallel in the same instance. This limitation means that you're better off distributing each request to its own instance so requests complete as quickly as possible without contention. In this scenario, set a low [HTTP trigger concurrency](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-functions/functions-concurrency#http-trigger-concurrency) of `1`. With 10 concurrent requests, the app scales to a steady state of roughly 10 instances, and each instance is continuously active processing one request at a time.\n  Because the size of each instance is ~2 GB, the consumption for a single continuously active instance is `2 GB * 3600 s = 7200 GB-s`. Assuming an on-demand execution rate of $0.000026 GB-s (without any free grants applied), the cost becomes `$0.1872 USD` per hour per instance. Because the CPU-bound app scales to 10 instances, the total hourly rate for execution time is `$1.872 USD`.\n  Similarly, the on-demand per-execution charge (without any free grants) of 40 requests per second is equal to `40 * 3600 = 144,000` or `0.144 million` executions per hour. Assuming an on-demand rate of `$0.40` per million executions, the total (grant-free) hourly cost of executions is `0.144 * $0.40`, which is `$0.0576` per hour.\n  In this scenario, the total hourly cost of running on-demand on 10 instances is `$1.872 + $0.0576s = $1.9296 USD`.\n2. **IO bound workload:** In an IO-bound workload, most of the application time is spent waiting on incoming request, which might be limited by network throughput or other upstream factors. Because of the limited inputs, the code can process multiple operations concurrently without negative impacts. In this scenario, assume you can process all 10 concurrent requests on the same instance.\n  Because consumption charges are based only on the memory of each active instance, the consumption charge calculation is simply `2 GB * 3600 s = 7200 GB-s`, which at the assumed on-demand execution rate (without any free grants applied) is `$0.1872 USD` per hour for the single instance.\n  As in the CPU-bound scenario, the on-demand per-execution charge (without any free grants) of 40 requests per second is equal to `40 * 3600 = 144,000` or 0.144 million executions per hour. In this case, the total (grant-free) hourly cost of executions `0.144 * $0.40`, which is `$0.0576` per hour.\n  In this scenario, the total hourly cost of running on-demand a single instance is `$0.1872 + $0.0576 = $0.245 USD`.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-functions/functions-consumption-costs#consumption-based-costs"},{"title":"Azure Functions on Azure Container Apps overview","content":"# Azure Functions on Azure Container Apps overview\n## Key benefits\nThe Container Apps hosting model builds on the flexibility of containerized workloads and the event-driven nature of Azure Functions. It offers the following key advantages:\n1. **Run Azure Functions as containers** with custom dependencies and language stacks.\n2. **Scale in to zero and scale out to 1000 instances** using KEDA.\n3. **[Secure networking](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/networking)** with full [VNet integration](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/custom-virtual-networks).\n4. **Advanced [Container App features](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/overview#features)** like multi-revisions, traffic splitting, [Dapr integration](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/dapr-overview), and [observability components](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/observability).\n5. **[Serverless and Dedicated GPU](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/gpu-serverless-overview)** support for compute-intensive workloads.\n6. **Unified Container Apps environment** to run Functions alongside microservices, APIs, and background jobs.\nThe following table helps you compare the features of Functions on Container Apps with [Flex consumption plan](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-functions/flex-consumption-plan).\n| Feature | Container Apps | Flex Consumption Plan| \n|  --- | --- | ---  |\n| Scale to zero | ✅ Yes (via KEDA) | ✅ Yes |\n| Max scale-out | 1,000 (default 10, configurable) | 1,000 |\n| Always-on instances | ✅ Yes (via `minReplicas`) | ✅ Yes (via always-ready instances) |\n| VNet integration | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes |\n| Custom container support | ✅ Yes (bring your own image) | ❌ Limited (no bring your own container) |\n| GPU support | ✅ Yes (via serverless GPU dedicated workload profile) | ❌ No |\n| Built-in features | Container Apps feature support. For instance, KEDA, Dapr, multi-revisions, mTLS, sidecars, ingress control and more | Functions-only features |\n| Billing model | Container Apps pricing: Consumption plan (vCPU, memory, requests) & Dedicated plan (workload profile based) | Execution-time + always-ready instances |\n\nFor a complete comparison of the Functions on Container Apps against Flex Consumption plan and all other plan and hosting types, see [Functions scale and hosting options](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-functions/functions-scale).","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/functions-overview#key-benefits"},{"title":"Reliability in Azure Container Apps","content":"# Reliability in Azure Container Apps\n## Resilience to availability zone failures\nYou don't incur extra charges beyond standard Container Apps pricing when you enable zone redundancy. You pay the same rates for compute resources, requests, and vCore seconds whether zone redundancy is enabled or not. For more information, see [Container Apps pricing](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/details/container-apps/) and [Container Apps billing](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/container-apps/billing).\n### Configure availability zone support\n1. **Create a zone-redundant Container Apps environment.** For deployment instructions that cover the Azure portal, the Azure CLI, and Azure PowerShell, see [Create a zone-redundant container app](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/container-apps/how-to-zone-redundancy).\n2. **Migrate to a zone-redundant deployment.** You can't enable zone redundancy on an existing Container Apps environment. To upgrade existing environments that aren't zone redundant, create a new environment with zone redundancy enabled in a supported region. Then redeploy your container apps.\n3. **Disable zone redundancy.** Zone redundancy can't be disabled after its enabled during environment creation. If you require a non-zone-redundant deployment, you must create a new environment without enabling the zone redundancy option or deploy to a region that doesn't support availability zones.\n4. **Verify zone redundancy.** You can use the Azure portal, the Azure CLI, and Azure PowerShell to [verify the zone redundancy status](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/container-apps/how-to-zone-redundancy#verify-zone-redundancy) of your environment.\n### Capacity planning and management\nIf an availability zone becomes unavailable, the Container Apps platform uses your scale rules to decide when to replace any lost replicas in that zone. It's important to configure your scale rules correctly so that the scheduler can make appropriate scheduling decisions.\nTo configure your scale rules properly, follow these principles:\n1. **Set a minimum number of replicas** that your application can tolerate. It might take a short period of time for lost replicas to be replaced because the platform must detect that the old replicas are gone. Then new replicas must start and return a healthy readiness probe status before they can receive incoming requests. If you can't tolerate any period with fewer than the minimum number of replicas that you've specified, consider [over-provisioning](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/reliability/concept-redundancy-replication-backup#manage-capacity-with-over-provisioning) to keep your application performant even if a zone becomes unavailable.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/reliability/reliability-container-apps#resilience-to-availability-zone-failures"},{"title":"Azure Functions on Azure Container Apps overview","content":"# Azure Functions on Azure Container Apps overview\n## Considerations\n9.3. [Use Azure API Management (APIM) to authenticate requests](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-functions/security-concepts#use-azure-api-management-apim-to-authenticate-requests)\n9.4. [Deploy your function app to a virtual network](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/custom-virtual-networks?tabs=workload-profiles-env)\n10. **Quota and resource limits**: Container Apps environments have default limits on memory, CPU, and instance counts per region. For more information, see the [environment limits](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/environment#limits-and-quotas) and [default quotas](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/quotas). If your workload requires more resources, you can [request a quota increase](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/quota-requests).\n11. **Manual scale rule configuration**: The \"Add scale rules\" button on the Azure portal is disabled for Azure Functions hosted on Container Apps because scaling rules are automatically configured based on the event source. Manual KEDA rule definitions aren't required in this setup.\n## Submit Feedback\nSubmit an issue or a feature request to the [Azure Container Apps GitHub repo](https://github.com/microsoft/azure-container-apps/issues).\n## Next Steps / Further Resources\nTo continue learning and building with Azure Functions on Container Apps, explore the following resources:\n1. [Getting started](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/functions-usage): Step-by-step guide to deploying and configuring Azure Functions in Azure Container Apps.\n2. [Azure Container Apps documentation](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/overview): Full reference for Container Apps features including scaling, networking, Dapr, and workload profiles.\n3. [Azure Container Apps pricing](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/details/container-apps/): Details on consumption-based billing and Dedicated plan costs.\n4. [Azure Functions hosting options](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-functions/functions-scale): Comparison of hosting plans including Container Apps, Flex Consumption, Premium, and Dedicated.\n5. [Azure Functions developer guide](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/azure-functions/functions-reference): Deep dive into triggers, bindings, runtime behavior, and configuration.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/container-apps/functions-overview#considerations"},{"title":"AKS on Windows Server pricing details","content":"# AKS on Windows Server pricing details\n## Pricing details\nAzure pricing for running workloads on AKS on Windows Server is based on US currency list pricing with:\n1. Pay-as-you-go pricing\n2. D-series general purpose VM sizes (D2s v4, D4s V4, and D8s V4)\n3. Standard HDD\n4. No uptime SLA (included in the support level)\nIn addition, AKS Arc pricing is based on the US currency list pricing with no discounts applied. The monthly price estimates are based on 730 hours of usage.\nFor detailed pricing information, see the [AKS pricing details](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/details/azure-stack/aks-hci/#overview) page. The list price for AKS Arc includes the following features:\n1. Kubernetes control plane and load balancer nodes:\n1.1. No charge for Arc-enabled AKS management cluster usage.\n1.2. No charge for workload cluster control plane and load balancer nodes.\n2. Linux (CBL-Mariner) container hosts:\n2.1. Pricing doesn't include Windows container hosts as they are licensed separately through regular licensing channels.\n2.2. Windows Server Standard: Unlimited Windows containers and two Hyper-V containers.\n2.3. Windows Server Datacenter: Unlimited Windows and Hyper-V containers.\n3. Azure Arc-enabled Kubernetes at no extra charge and the following items:\n3.1. **Inventory, grouping, and tagging** in Azure.\n3.2. **Deployment of apps and configurations with GitOps**: Included at no extra charge (normally, the initial six vCPUs are free, and then afterwards, the charged per vCPU per month).\n3.3. **Azure Policy for Kubernetes**: Included at no extra charge (normally, the charge per vCPU per cluster for each month).\n4. If you enable hyper-threading on the physical computer, this feature reduces the measured vCPU count by 50 percent.\nNote\nRunning Windows Server containers on AKS Arc requires a Windows Server license. You can acquire the license separately through regular licensing channels, or you can add it to the cost of running a Windows virtual machine on Azure. For users with Windows Server Software Assurance, [Azure Hybrid Benefits](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/aks/aksarc/azure-hybrid-benefit-22h2) might apply, thus reducing or eliminating the Windows Server license fees.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/aks/aksarc/pricing#pricing-details"}]}
```

```
{"results":[{"title":"Use an Azure free account to try Azure Database for PostgreSQL for free","content":"# Use an Azure free account to try Azure Database for PostgreSQL for free\nAzure Database for PostgreSQL is a managed service that you use to run, manage, and scale highly available PostgreSQL databases in the cloud. With an Azure free account, you can use an Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance for **free for 12 months** with **monthly limits** of up to:\n1. **750 hours** of **Burstable B1MS** instance, enough hours to run a database instance continuously each month.\n2. **32 GB storage and 32 GB backup storage**.\nThis article shows you how to create and use an Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance for free using an [Azure free account](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/purchase-options/azure-account?cid=msft_learn).\n## Prerequisites\nTo complete this tutorial, you need:\n1. An Azure free account. If you don't have one, [create a free account](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/purchase-options/azure-account?cid=msft_learn) before you begin.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account"},{"title":"Use an Azure free account to try Azure Database for PostgreSQL for free","content":"# Use an Azure free account to try Azure Database for PostgreSQL for free\n## Monitor and track free services usage\nYou're not charged for Azure Database for PostgreSQL services that are included for free with your Azure free account unless you exceed the free service limits. To remain within the limits, use the Azure portal to track and monitor your free services usage.\n1. In the Azure portal, search for **Subscriptions** and select the Azure free account - **Free Trial** subscription.\n2. On the **Overview** page, scroll down to show the tile **Top free services by usage**, and then select **View all free services**.\n [![Screenshot that shows the Free Trial subscription overview page and highlights View all free services.](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/media/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account/free-services-usage-overview.png)](media/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account/free-services-usage-overview.png#lightbox)\n3. Locate the below meters related to **Azure Database for PostgreSQL** to track usage:\n | Meter | Description | Monthly Limit |\n | --- | --- | --- |\n | Azure Database for PostgreSQL, Flexible Server Burstable BS Series Compute, B1MS | Tracks Compute usage in terms of number of hours of running | 750 Hours per month - Burstable B1MS Compute Tier |\n | Azure Database for PostgreSQL, Flexible Server Storage, Data Stored | Tracks Data Storage Provisioned in terms of GB used per month | 32 GB per month |\n [![Screenshot that shows the View and track usage information blade on Azure portal for all free services.](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/media/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account/free-services-tracking.png)](media/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account/free-services-tracking.png#lightbox)\n3.1. Meter: Identifies the unit of measure for the service being consumed.\n3.2. Usage/Limit: Current month's usage and limit for the meter.\n3.3. Status: Usage status of the service. Based on your usage, you can have one of the following statutes:\n3.4. Not in use: You haven't used the meter or the usage for the meter hasn't reached the billing system.\n3.5. Exceeded on <Date>: You've exceeded the limit for the meter on <Date>.\n3.6. Unlikely to Exceed: You're unlikely to exceed the limit for the meter.\n3.7. Exceeds on <Date>: You're likely to exceed the limit for the meter on <Date>.\nImportant","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account#monitor-and-track-free-services-usage"},{"title":"Use an Azure free account to try Azure Database for PostgreSQL for free","content":"# Use an Azure free account to try Azure Database for PostgreSQL for free\n## Create a flexible server instance\n | Availability zone | No preference | If your application (hosted on Azure VMs, virtual machine scale sets or AKS instance) is provisioned in a specific availability zone, create your flexible server instance in the same availability zone. Collocating the application and database improves performance by reducing network latency across zones. If you choose **No preference**, a default AZ is selected for you. |\n | High availability | Default | Leave the High Availability option unchecked. |\n | Authentication method | PostgreSQL authentication only | Select the authentication method that suits your authentication needs for this instance of PostgreSQL. |\n | Admin username | myadmin | Create a sign-in account to use when you connect to the server. The admin username must contain between 1 and 63 characters, must only contain numbers and letters, can't start with **pg\\_** and can't be **azure\\_superuser**, **azure\\_pg\\_admin**, **admin**, **administrator**, **root**, **guest**, or **public**. |\n | Password | Your password | Specify a password for the server admin account. The password must contain between 8 and 128 characters. It must also contain characters from three of the following four categories: English uppercase letters, English lowercase letters, numbers (0 through 9), and non-alphanumeric characters (!, $, #, %, and so on). Your password cannot contain all or part of the login name. Part of a login name is defined as three or more consecutive alphanumeric characters. |\n5. For **Compute + storage** setting, keep the default values populated upon selecting **Development** workload type.\n ![Screenshot that shows the default values for compute + storage settings.](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/media/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account/compute-storage-default-postgresql.png)\n Select **Configure Server** to review and customize the **Compute + storage** setting.\n Make sure to select Burstable B1MS Instance (1-2 vCores), specify to include storage of less than or equal to 32 GB, and keep the default settings for the remaining options.\n [![Screenshot that shows the Compute + Storage Configure Server blade, to choose B1MS SKU and 32GB Storage.](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/media/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account/compute-storage-postgresql.png)](media/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account/compute-storage-postgresql.png#lightbox)\n Select **Save** to continue with the configuration.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account#create-a-flexible-server-instance"},{"title":"Use an Azure free account to try Azure Database for PostgreSQL for free","content":"# Use an Azure free account to try Azure Database for PostgreSQL for free\n## Create a flexible server instance\nWhen creating the Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance using your Azure free account, you will see an **Estimated cost per month** in the **Compute + Storage: Cost Summary** blade and the **Review + Create** tab. However, as long as you stay within the monthly limits of your Azure free account (refer to the **Monitor and track free services usage** section below for usage information). There is no charge for the service. We are currently working to improve the **Cost Summary** experience for free services.\n11. Select **Create** to provision the server.\nProvisioning can take a few minutes.\n12. On the toolbar, select **Notifications** (the bell icon) to monitor the deployment process.\nAfter the deployment is complete, select **Pin to dashboard**, to create a tile for the Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance on your Azure portal dashboard. This tile is a shortcut to the server's **Overview** page. When you select **Go to resource**, the server's **Overview** page opens.\nBy default, a **postgres** database is created under your server. The postgres database is a default database that's meant for use by users, utilities, and third-party applications. (The other default database is **azure\\_maintenance**. Its function is to separate the managed service processes from user actions. You can't access this database.)\n## Connect and query\nNow that you've created an Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance in a resource group, you can connect to server and query databases by using the following Connect and query quickstarts:\n1. [psql](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/quickstart-create-server#connect-using-psql).\n2. [Azure CLI](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/connectivity/connect-azure-cli).\n3. [Python](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/connectivity/connect-python).\n4. [Java](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/connectivity/connect-java).\n5. [.NET](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/connectivity/connect-csharp).\n6. [Connect to a server in VNET](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/connectivity/quickstart-create-connect-server-vnet).","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account#create-a-flexible-server-instance"},{"title":"Use an Azure free account to try Azure Database for PostgreSQL for free","content":"# Use an Azure free account to try Azure Database for PostgreSQL for free\n## Monitor and track free services usage\nWith an Azure free account, you also get $200 in credit to use in 30 days. During this time, any usage beyond the free monthly amounts of services will be deducted from this credit. At the end of your first 30 days or after you spend your $200 credit (whichever comes first), you'll only pay for what you use beyond the free monthly amounts of services. To keep getting free services after 30 days, move to pay-as-you-go pricing. If you don't move to pay as you go, you can't purchase Azure services beyond your $200 credit—and eventually your account and services will be disabled. For more information, see [**Azure free account FAQ**](https://azure.microsoft.com/free/free-account-faq/).\n## Clean up resources\nIf you're using the Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance for development, testing, or predictable, time-bound production workloads, optimize usage by starting and stopping the server on-demand. After you stop the server, it remains in that state for seven days unless restarted sooner. For more information, see Start/Stop Server to lower TCO. When your Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance is stopped, there's no Compute usage, but Storage usage is still considered.\nAlternatively, if you don't expect to need these resources in the future, you can delete them by deleting the resource group, or you can just delete the Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance.\n1. To delete the resource group, complete the following steps:\n1.1. In the Azure portal, search for and select **Resource groups**.\n1.2. In the list of resource groups, select the name of your resource group.\n1.3. On the **Overview** page for your resource group, select **Delete resource group.**\n1.4. In the confirmation dialog box, type the name of your resource group, and then select **Delete**.\n2. To create an Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance, search for and select **Azure Database for PostgreSQL servers**:\n ![Screenshot that shows how to search and select Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance.](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/media/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account/select-postgresql.png)\n Alternatively, you can search for and navigate to **Free Services**, and then select **Azure Database for PostgreSQL** tile from the list:\n3. Select **Create**.\n4. Enter the basic settings for a new **Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server** instance.\n [![Screenshot that shows the Basic Settings for creating an Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance.](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/media/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account/basic-settings-postgresql.png)](media/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account/basic-settings-postgresql.png#lightbox)\n | Setting | Suggested Value | Description |\n | --- | --- | --- |\n | Subscription | Your subscription name | Select the Free Trial Azure subscription. |\n | Resource group | Your resource group | Enter a new resource group or an existing one from your subscription. |\n | Server name | mydemoserver-pgsql | Specify a unique name to identify your flexible server instance. The domain name *postgres.database.azure.com* is appended to the server name you provide. The server name can contain only lowercase letters, numbers, and the hyphen (-) character. It must contain between 3 and 63 characters. |\n | Region | The region closest to your users | Select a location from the list, preferably the location that's closest to your users. |\n | PostgreSQL version | The latest major version | Use the latest PostgreSQL major version unless you have specific requirements otherwise. |\n | Workload type | Development | For a free trial, select **Development** workload. For a production workload, choose Small/Medium-size or Large-size depending on your requirements. |\n | Compute + storage | Default | Select the SKU of the compute and storage that better accommodates your performance and cost needs. |","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/how-to-deploy-on-azure-free-account#create-a-flexible-server-instance"},{"title":"Quickstart: Create an Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server","content":"# Quickstart: Create an Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server\nAzure Database for PostgreSQL is a managed service that you can use to run, manage, and scale highly available PostgreSQL databases in the cloud.\nThis quickstart shows you how to create an Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance by using the Azure portal, Azure CLI, or Azure Resource Manager (ARM) templates.\n## Prerequisites\nBefore you begin, make sure you have:\n1. An Azure subscription. If you don't have one, create a [free Azure account](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/purchase-options/azure-account?cid=msft_learn).\n2. Appropriate permissions to create resources in your subscription.\n## Understand what you're creating\nAn Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance includes:\n1. A configured set of [compute and storage resources](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/concepts-compute).\n2. Deployment within an [Azure resource group](https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/azure/azure-resource-manager/management/overview).\n3. A `postgres` database created by default.\n4. An `azure_maintenance` database for managed service processes.\n5. An `azure_sys` database for query store and autonomous tuning features.\nNote\nConnections typically use port 5432, or port 6432 if connecting through the built-in [PgBouncer](https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/connectivity/concepts-pgbouncer) connection pooler.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/configure-maintain/quickstart-create-server"},{"title":"Storage in Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server","content":"# Storage in Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server\n## Differences between Premium SSD and Premium SSD v2\n| Disk size | Premium SSD IOPS | Premium SSD v2 IOPS| \n|  --- | --- | ---  |\n| 32 GiB | Provisioned 120; up to 3,500 | First 3000 IOPS free can scale up to 17179 |\n| 64 GiB | Provisioned 240; up to 3,500 | First 3000 IOPS free can scale up to 34359 |\n| 128 GiB | Provisioned 500; up to 3,500 | First 3000 IOPS free can scale up to 68719 |\n| 256 GiB | Provisioned 1,100; up to 3,500 | First 3000 IOPS free can scale up to 80000 |\n| 512 GiB | Provisioned 2,300; up to 3,500 | First 12000 IOPS free can scale to 80000 |\n| 1 TiB | 5,000 | First 12000 IOPS free can scale up to 80000 |\n| 2 TiB | 7,500 | First 12000 IOPS free can scale up to 80000 |\n| 4 TiB | 7,500 | First 12000 IOPS free can scale up to 80000 |\n| 8 TiB | 16,000 | First 12000 IOPS free can scale up to 80000 |\n| 16 TiB | 18,000 | First 12000 IOPS free can scale up to 80000 |\n| 32 TiB | 20,000 | First 12000 IOPS free can scale up to 80000 |\n| 64 TiB | N/A | First 12000 IOPS free can scale up to 80000 |\n\nThe following table provides an overview of premium SSD v2 disk capacities and performance maximums to help you decide which want you should use.\n| SSD v2 disk size | Maximum available IOPS | Maximum available throughput (MB/s)| \n|  --- | --- | ---  |\n| 1 GiB-64 TiBs | 3,000-80,000 (Increases by 500 IOPS per GiB) | 125-1,200 (increases by 0.25 MB/s per set IOPS) |\n","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/compute-storage/concepts-storage#differences-between-premium-ssd-and-premium-ssd-v2"},{"title":"Create services included with Azure free account","content":"# Create services included with Azure free account\nDuring the first 30 days after you've created an Azure free account, you have $200 credit in your billing currency to use on any service, except for third-party Marketplace purchases. You can experiment with different tiers and types of Azure services using the free credit to try out Azure. If you use services or Azure resources that aren’t free during that time, charges are deducted against your credit.\nIf you don’t use all of your credit by the end of the first 30 days, it's lost. After the first 30 days and up to 12 months after sign-up, you can only use a limited quantity of *some services*—not all Azure services are free. If you upgrade before 30 days and have remaining credit, you can use the rest of your credit with a pay-as-you-go subscription for the remaining days. For example, if you sign up for the free account on November 1 and upgrade on November 5, you have until November 30 to use your credit in the new pay-as-you-go subscription.\nYour Azure free account includes a *specified quantity* of free services for 12 months and a set of services that are always free. Only some tiers of services are available for free within certain quantities. For example, Azure has many virtual machines intended for different needs. The free account includes access to three types of VMs for free—the B1S, B2pts v2 (ARM-based), and B2ats v2 (AMD-based) burstable VMs that are usable for up to 750 hours per month. By staying in the free account limits, you can use the free services in various configurations. For more information about the Azure free account and the products that are available for free, see [Azure free account FAQ](https://azure.microsoft.com/pricing/purchase-options/azure-account?cid=msft_learn).","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/cost-management-billing/manage/create-free-services"},{"title":"Storage in Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server","content":"# Storage in Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server\n## Differences between Premium SSD and Premium SSD v2\nUnlike Premium SSD, Premium SSD v2 doesn't have dedicated sizes. You can set a Premium SSD v2 disk to any size you prefer, and make granular adjustments as per your workload requirements. Those granular increments can go in steps of 1 GiB. Premium SSD v2 doesn't support host caching, but still provide lower latency than Premium SSD. Premium SSD v2 capacities range from 1 GiB to 64 TiBs.\nThe following table provides a comparison of different aspect of the types of disk supported by Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instances, to help you decide which one suits your needs better.\n| - | Premium SSD v2 | Premium SSD| \n|  --- | --- | ---  |\n| **Disk type** | SSD | SSD |\n| **Scenario** | Production and performance-sensitive workloads that consistently require low latency and high IOPS and throughput. | Production and performance-sensitive workloads. |\n| **Max disk size** | 65,536 GiB | 32,767 GiB |\n| **Max throughput** | 1,200 MB/s | 900 MB/s |\n| **Max IOPS** | 80,000 | 20,000 |\n\nPremium SSD v2 offers up to 32 TiBs per region per subscription by default, but supports higher capacity by request. To request an increase in capacity, request a quota increase or contact [Azure Support](https://ms.portal.azure.com/#view/Microsoft_Azure_Support/HelpAndSupportBlade/%7E/overview).\nAzure Database for PostgreSQL service offers a baseline throughput of 125 MB/s for disks up to 399 GiB, and 500 MB/s for disks over 400 GiB at no extra cost. Increasing throughput beyond the free tier results in extra charges.\nThe storage that you provision is the amount of storage capacity available to your Azure Database for PostgreSQL flexible server instance. This storage is used for database files, temporary files, transaction logs, and PostgreSQL server logs. The total amount of storage that you provision also defines the I/O capacity available to your server.","contentUrl":"https://learn.microsoft.com/azure/postgresql/compute-storage/concepts-storage#differences-between-premium-ssd-and-premium-ssd-v2"}]}
```

> AGENT

PR #598 is merged and `main` is up to date. Now the Azure question — I verified the terms against Microsoft's current docs so this isn't from memory. Short version: **day 30 is only a cliff if you don't upgrade; the "1 year" part only kicks in if you do.**

## How the offer actually works

The free account is two separate things:

1. **$200 credit, first 30 days only.** Everything you run is burned against it. Unused credit is lost at day 30 — it never carries past that date, even if you upgrade.
2. **12 months of limited free service quantities** (+ some always-free services). This is the "1 year" — but it only continues **if you upgrade to pay-as-you-go**. Upgrading requires adding a card; you're then charged only for usage *beyond* the free quantities.

**If you do nothing at day 30:** the subscription is **disabled** — frontend, api, worker, Redis, Postgres, ADF polling all stop at once. Resources aren't deleted immediately (there's a grace window where upgrading re-enables them), but the app is effectively dead. One important survivor: **the Entra tenant is not part of the subscription** — the demo users and app registrations keep working, so Azure-AD login for local dev survives even a disabled subscription.

## What DataQ would cost on pay-as-you-go

| DataQ piece | After upgrade |
|---|---|
| Postgres (`dataq-pg-wus3-*`, shared app+harness) | **Free for 12 months** if it's B1MS with ≤32GB — 750 h/month covers 24/7. This is the big one; worth confirming the SKU |
| Container Apps ×4 (frontend, api, worker, redis) | Always-free grant: 180K vCPU-s + 360K GiB-s + 2M requests/month per subscription. A month is ~2.6M seconds, so our always-on worker+redis exceed the grant → the main real cost. Idle-rate billing helps (a quiet worker between beats bills reduced); ballpark **$10–40/month**, less if api scales to zero and sizes are trimmed |
| App Insights / Log Analytics | First 5GB/month ingestion free — our volume is likely within it |
| Key Vault, ADLS, ADF (harness) | Pennies; ADF is per-activity-run — pause the harness triggers if not demoing |

## What this means for the v1.1 plan

The W1–3 sequencing treated ~07-25 as a hard stop. Upgrading changes the calculus:

- **If you upgrade (~$10–40/month):** the Azure deadline disappears. Prod stays live through the whole cycle — which means live E2E for the schema_drift/anomaly/scale weeks too — and W3's #590 becomes a **cost-trim pass** (scale-to-zero, pause harness ADF, prune ingestion) instead of `terraform destroy`. Bonus: upgrading lifts the trial quota caps (the 1-env/1-PG-server limit that forced the shared-server design in ADR 0024). If you go this way: set a **Cost Management budget + alert** (say $30/month) immediately — PAYG has no spend cap.
- **If you don't:** the current plan is exactly right — everything cloud-verified by end of W3, deliberate teardown before the disable hits, and note that whatever's left of the $200 evaporates at day 30 regardless (an argument for *spending* it on the #587 volume baseline and W2 live verification rather than conserving it).

My take: the Snowflake urgency is unchanged either way (separate subscription, days left). For Azure, if a small monthly bill is acceptable, upgrading before day 30 is clearly better value than racing the teardown — but it's a real card-on-file decision, so it's yours. If you decide, I'll adjust #590/#591 and the W3 milestone accordingly.

```
# Azure Container Apps pricing

- [Request a pricing quote](/en-us/contact/pricing/)
- [Try Azure for free](/en-us/free/)

1. Overview
2. Pricing table
3. Purchase options
4. Resources
5. FAQ
6. [Azure Container Apps](/en-us/products/container-apps/)
7. 
8. [Free account](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/free/)

## Build and deploy modern apps and microservices using serverless containers

 Azure Container Apps is a fully managed serverless container service that scales dynamically based on HTTP traffic or events. 

 ﻿ 

## Explore pricing options

- ### Pay as you go

Pay for compute capacity by the second, with no long-term commitments or upfront payments. Increase or decrease consumption on demand.

[Learn more](/en-us/pricing/purchase-options/pay-as-you-go/)
- ### Azure savings plan for compute

Save money across select compute services globally by committing to spend a fixed hourly amount for 1 or 3 years, unlocking lower prices until you reach your hourly commitment. Suited for dynamic workloads while accommodating for planned or unplanned changes.

[Learn more](/en-us/pricing/offers/savings-plan-compute/)

Apply filters to customize pricing options to your needs.

Prices are estimates only and are not intended as actual price quotes. Actual pricing may vary depending on the type of agreement entered with Microsoft, date of purchase, and the currency exchange rate. Prices are calculated based on US dollars and converted using London closing spot rates that are captured in the two business days prior to the last business day of the previous month end. If the two business days prior to the end of the month fall on a bank holiday in major markets, the rate setting day is generally the day immediately preceding the two business days. This rate applies to all transactions during the upcoming month. Sign in to the [Azure pricing calculator](/en-us/pricing/calculator/) to see pricing based on your current program/offer with Microsoft. Contact an [Azure sales specialist](/en-us/contact/pricing/) for more information on pricing or to request a price quote. See [frequently asked questions](/en-us/pricing/) about Azure pricing.

US government entities are eligible to purchase Azure Government services from a licensing solution provider with no upfront financial commitment, or directly through a pay-as-you-go online subscription.

[Learn more](/en-us/explore/global-infrastructure/government/)

Important—The price in R$ is merely a reference; this is an international transaction and the final price is subject to exchange rates and the inclusion of IOF taxes. An eNF will not be issued.

# Consumption plan

 Azure Container Apps consumption plan is billed based on per-second resource allocation and requests. The first 180,000 vCPU-seconds, 360,000 GiB-seconds, and 2 million requests per subscription per month are free. Beyond that, you pay for what you use on a per second basis based on the number of vCPU-s and GiB-s your applications are allocated. 

Applications scale on-demand based on requests and events. Container Apps replicas are billed for active usage when they are running. An application can be configured to scale to zero replicas when there are no requests or events to process. No usage charges apply when an application is scaled to zero.

You can optionally configure Container Apps with a minimum number of replicas to be always running in idle mode. When an application scales down to its minimum number of replicas, usage is charged at a reduced idle rate when a replica is inactive. A replica enters active mode and is charged at the active rate when it is starting up, when it is processing requests, or when its vCPU or bandwidth usage are above the active billing thresholds^1^.

For Container Apps jobs, resources consumed by each execution are billed at the active rate from its start to completion. No usage charges apply when a job is not running executions.

Resources consumed by managed Java components are billed at the active/idle rates. You may delete components that are no longer in use to stop incurring charges related to those components.

Azure Container Apps Express and Sandboxes follow the same pay-per-second pricing as Consumption Plan.

## Resource consumption

### Active usage

^1^A replica is active when vCPU usage is above 0.01 cores or when data received is above 1,000 bytes per second.

| Meter | Pay as you go Price\* | 1-year Savings Plan Price\* | 3-year Savings Plan Price\* |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| vCPU (seconds) | $- per second | $- per second | $- per second |
| Memory (GiB-Seconds) | $- per second | $- per second | $- per second |
^\*^All prices are after Free Grant (per Month) of 180,000 vCPU-seconds and 360,000 GiB-seconds.

### Serverless GPU

 Consumption-based serverless GPUs allow near real-time inferencing for custom models, SLM fine-tuning and other machine learning tasks with instant serverless scale that goes down to zero when not in use.

| Meter | Pay as you go Price\* | 1-year Savings Plan Price\* | 3-year Savings Plan Price\* |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| NC T4 v3 | $- per second | $- per second | $- per second |
| NC A100 v4 | $- per second | $- per second | $- per second |
^\*^The GPU prices shown above are in addition to the active usage vCPU and RAM prices for your Container Apps.

### Idle usage

| Meter | Pay as you go Price\* | 1-year Savings Plan Price\* | 3-year Savings Plan Price\* |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| vCPU (seconds) | $- per second | $- per second | $- per second |
| Memory (GiB-Seconds) | $- per second | $- per second | $- per second |
^\*^All prices are after Free Grant (per Month) of 180,000 vCPU-seconds and 360,000 GiB-seconds.

## Requests

Container Apps are billed based on the total number of requests^2^ processed each month. The first two million requests are included free per subscription per month.

| Meter | Pay as you go price | 1 year savings plan | 3 year savings plan |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Requests | $- per million | $- per million | $- per million |
^2^A request is a single HTTP request received by an app. For TCP enabled apps, a request is a single TCP connection. Request charges do not apply to jobs.^\*^All prices are after Free Grant (per Month) of 2 Million requests. 

## Dynamic sessions

Dynamic sessions in Azure Container Apps enable you to run untrusted code securely at scale.

| Meter | Pay as you go price | 1 year savings plan | 3 year savings plan |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Platform-managed built-in container (Python, Node.js, Shell) | $- per session-hour | $- per session-hour | $- per session-hour |

# Dedicated plan

Azure Container Apps Dedicated plan provides a single tenancy guarantee, access to specialized hardware, and more predictable pricing. Billing for the Dedicated plan is based on the number of vCPU seconds and gibibyte (GiB) seconds allocated across instances. In addition, you are billed a base price for the Dedicated plan management.

When creating apps in the Dedicated plan you can choose from several workload profiles, each with different amounts of vCPUs and GiBs of memory. You will be billed for the total number of vCPUs and memory provisioned in this workload profile, per second that each instance is running. More than one app can run in a single workload profile depending on how many resources each app requires and the resources available in the workload profile. These workload profiles can automatically scale out to multiple instances as needed.

| Meter | Pay as you go price | 1 year savings plan | 3 year savings plan |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Management (hour)\* | $- per hour | $- per hour | $- per hour |
| vCPU (hour) | $- per hour | $- per hour | $- per hour |
| Memory (GiB-hour) | $- per hour | $- per hour | $- per hour |
| GPU (hour) | $- per hour | $- per hour | $- per hour |
^\*^This meter applies to the Dedicated plan, private endpoint, and planned maintenance. Charges are additive, if multiple features are used within an environment, charges will apply to each feature individually. 

*vCPU and memory prices do not apply to GPU workload profiles.*

*Dynamic sessions in Azure Container Apps dedicated plan has no premium or additional charges outside of the Azure Container Apps charges.*

## Azure pricing and purchasing options

[Frequently asked questions about Azure pricing](/en-us/pricing/)

- Yes. You can scale to zero and only pay for when your app is active in response to events and/or requests. You can also optionally choose to keep a minimum capacity always running in idle mode to optimize response times based on your needs. This capacity is charged at a reduced rate when you are not processing any requests.
- The first 180,000 vCPU-seconds, 360,000 GiB-seconds, and 2 million requests each month are free. Beyond that, you pay for what you use on a per second basis based on the number of vCPU-s and GiB-s your applications consume.
- Active usage occurs while your container is starting or while there is at least one request being processed by the application. By default, applications scale to zero. You can also configure Container Apps with a minimum number of instances so they’re always running in idle mode. Idle usage is charged at a reduced rate when the application isn’t processing any requests.
```

> AGENT

I now have the full picture from the actual Terraform (app stack + harness). One caveat up front: the pricing page hides exact numbers behind JavaScript, so I'm using the standard US-region ACA consumption list rates (≈$63/month per always-active vCPU, ≈$7.90/month per always-idle vCPU, ≈$7.90/month per GiB — idle discount applies to CPU only, memory bills the same either way). Also note: **the local `az` CLI is logged in as demo user Olivia** (leftover from the go-live workout) with Reader on one resource only, so I couldn't pull your real meter data — these are computed from the deployed sizes.

## What's actually deployed (from Terraform)

**App stack — all `min_replicas ≥ 1`, so always allocated:**

| App | Size | Always-on |
|---|---|---|
| `dataq-app-worker` (Celery + beat) | 1.0 vCPU / 2 GiB | yes (beat can't scale to zero) |
| `dataq-app-api` | 0.5 / 1 GiB (max 3) | yes |
| `dataq-app-redis` | 0.5 / 1 GiB | yes |
| `dataq-app-frontend` | 0.25 / 0.5 GiB (max 2) | yes |
| migrate job | 0.5 / 1 GiB | per-run, negligible |

Total: **2.25 vCPU / 4.5 GiB allocated 24/7.**

**Harness adds — this is the surprise:**

| App | Size | Always-on |
|---|---|---|
| Airflow scheduler+webserver | 2.0 vCPU / 4 GiB | yes |
| Airflow workers | 2.0 / 4 GiB **× min 2** | yes — **4.0 vCPU / 8 GiB** |
| harness redis | 0.5 / 1 GiB | yes |
| mockdata cron jobs, ADF (2 triggers), Event Grid, ADLS | — | pennies |

Total: **+6.5 vCPU / +13 GiB always allocated — the harness is ~3× the app's compute.**

## Monthly cost estimate (pay-as-you-go, after free grants)

| Scenario | All-idle (best) | Realistic† | All-active (worst) |
|---|---|---|---|
| App only (harness off) | ~$50 | **~$100–140** | ~$175 |
| Harness only | ~$155 | **~$350–450** | ~$515 |
| **Total, harness on** | ~$205 | **~$450–560** | ~$690 |

†"Realistic" assumes the Celery worker, both redises, the Airflow scheduler and its 2 workers bill *active* (constant broker polling/heartbeats usually exceed the 1,000 bytes/s idle threshold), while api/frontend/webserver sit idle. The idle-vs-active classification is the single biggest uncertainty — only the real bill settles it.

Everything else is roughly free: **Postgres is $0** (`B_Standard_B1ms` + 32 GB is exactly the 12-month free quantity, 750 h/month), Log Analytics likely under the 5 GB free, ADF ~$2–5, KV/Event Grid/storage/bandwidth ~$1–2.

## The urgent implication

If the harness has been fully up since deploy, burn is plausibly **$15–20/day — meaning the $200 credit could run out in days, well before the 30-day mark** (services get disabled at credit exhaustion, not at day 30). I'd check the real balance today: portal → Subscriptions → your Free Trial → credit remaining. If you `az login` as the owner account, I can pull Cost Management month-to-date and replace these estimates with actuals.

## The levers, in order of impact

1. **Scale the harness to zero when not demoing** (Airflow workers min 2→0, scheduler/webserver min 1→0, harness redis off): saves ~$350–450/month — by far the biggest lever, and reversible in one `az containerapp update` / TF variable per app.
2. **Trim the app**: api min 1→0 (cold starts behind the nginx proxy are the tradeoff), worker 1.0→0.5 vCPU (keep 2 GiB for GX/pandas headroom): app drops to roughly **$50–75/month** — that's the realistic steady-state bill if you upgrade to PAYG and keep prod alive through the cycle.
3. Requests never matter here (2M/month free, health probes don't count).

So the upgrade decision from earlier now has real numbers: **harness-off + trimmed app ≈ $50–75/month** to keep prod alive past day 30; harness-on continuously is ~$500/month and isn't worth it — wake it for demo/E2E windows only. Want me to fold a "scale-to-zero harness + app trim" task into #590/#591?

> AGENT

Full actuals now, from Cost Management (your billing currency is CAD). The picture is sharp:

## Real burn — CAD $21.69/day average (July 1–3), $144.80 consumed since the June 27 deploy

| Resource | CAD/day | Share |
|---|---|---|
| `dataq-harness-airflow` (scheduler+webserver, 2 vCPU/4Gi) | 8.99 | ← biggest single line |
| `dataq-harness-airflow-worker` (2× 2 vCPU/4Gi) | 6.92 | |
| `dataq-harness-redis` + `airflow-trigger` + ADF | 1.50 | |
| **Harness subtotal** | **~17.41** | **80%** |
| `dataq-app-worker` / `api` / `redis` / `frontend` | 4.04 | 19% |
| ACR (Basic, base fee) | 0.24 | |
| Postgres B1ms, Key Vault, logs, storage, mockdata jobs, alerts | **0.00** | free tiers doing their job |

So monthly: **harness ON ≈ CAD $660/mo (~USD $480)** · **harness OFF ≈ CAD $130/mo (~USD $95)** — my Terraform-based estimate held up, and the split answers your question directly: **the harness is 80% of the bill; the app itself is ~$4/day.**

## The runway problem

The Canada-localized free credit is normally **CAD $260** (the "$200" USD grant — please confirm the exact balance in portal → Subscriptions → Free Trial, the API doesn't expose it). If so:

- **Remaining ≈ CAD $115** → at $21.7/day the credit dies **~July 9–10** — not "3 weeks from now." The subscription gets disabled at credit exhaustion, not at day 30.
- **Scale the harness down today** and burn drops to ~$4.3/day → the same $115 lasts **~26 days**, comfortably past the day-30 mark (~July 26). The W1–3 plan timeline survives without paying anything.

## Recommendation

Stop the four harness apps now (`az containerapp stop` on `dataq-harness-airflow`, `-airflow-worker`, `-airflow-trigger`, `-redis` — fully reversible with `start` for demo/E2E windows). Two caveats: DataQ's 10-min Airflow polling will log connection failures while it's down (possible #419 alert noise — snooze or disable the Airflow connection's suites), and the Flow B/C harness legs are dead until restarted. ADF ($0.27/day) can stay.

This also refines the earlier upgrade math: post-upgrade PAYG steady state is **~CAD $130/mo as-deployed, likely ~CAD $60–80 trimmed** (api scale-to-zero, worker 0.5 vCPU), with the harness woken only when needed.

```
<system-reminder>This memory is 5 days old. Memories are point-in-time observations, not live state — claims about code behavior or file:line citations may be outdated. Verify against current code before asserting as fact.</system-reminder>
1	- [DataQ — Orchestration providers, not datasources](dataq_adf_is_orchestration.md) — ADF + Airflow share an OrchestrationProvider abstraction; monitor + detect failure + trigger suite on success only; never queryable datasources
2	- [DataQ — Working agreements](feedback_working_agreements.md) — single-functionality commits, manual test before next feature, GitHub issues for defects (no silent fixes), conda + Black for Python, locked coding structure
3	- [DataQ — /review before merge](feedback_review_before_merge.md) — spawn the /code-review AGENTS (never an inline self-review) on every PR; any deferred finding must be filed/noted, never silently dropped
4	- [DataQ — Verify locally before push](feedback_verify_locally_before_push.md) — run format/lint/typecheck/**bandit**/tests locally before commit; don't use CI as the first feedback loop (Ruff noqa ≠ Bandit B105)
5	- [DataQ — Fixup commits for in-review PRs](feedback_fixup_commits_in_review.md) — revise open PRs with a follow-up `fixup:` commit + plain push, never amend + force-push (squash-merge collapses it anyway)
6	- [DataQ — Test failure modes, not happy paths](feedback_test_failure_modes.md) — adversarial-input battery for data-ingesting code (backend/tests/support/adversarial.py); don't mock the seam under test; periodic mutmut spikes; ~94% line coverage still missed profiler 500s (#145/#147)
7	- [DataQ — GitHub hygiene: labels + milestones](feedback_issue_milestone.md) — every issue gets label(s) + milestone; every PR gets a milestone (feature-week; labels aren't a PR convention); closed-week milestones need the API-by-number set, not `gh pr edit`
8	- [DataQ — Local shell is Fish](dev-shell-is-fish.md) — Bash-tool commands run under Fish: quote glob flags (`--include="*.md"`), no `timeout` on macOS (use run_in_background), avoid `cd &&` chaining
9	- [DataQ — No creds in tracked files](feedback_no_creds_in_tracked_files.md) — never commit a credential (even mock/local); env templates ship blank, generate the local-dev password in setup.sh into the gitignored .env
10	- [DataQ — gh poisoned-cache 401](dev-gh-poisoned-cache-401.md) — `gh pr checks/merge` 401 "Bad credentials" while `gh api` works = stale entry in `~/.cache/gh`; rm the "Bad credentials" file, don't swap to REST
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/arijit/Coding/Python/DataQ-harness/scripts/harness_window.sh",
  "content": "#!/usr/bin/env bash\n# harness_window.sh — open/close an ad-hoc DataQ harness test window on Azure.\n#\n# The harness compute was stopped 2026-07-04 (cost wind-down, DataQ #590): while\n# awake it burns ~CAD 17/day (~0.70/hour); stopped it costs ~0. This script wakes\n# it, optionally kicks the data flows, and puts it back to sleep.\n#\n# Usage:\n#   harness_window.sh status           # show app/trigger/job states (read-only)\n#   harness_window.sh start            # wake: redis -> airflow -> worker -> trigger, ADF triggers on, jobs resumed\n#   harness_window.sh run [--adf]      # kick the 5 mockdata jobs (+ optionally the Flow-A ADF pipelines*)\n#   harness_window.sh stop             # sleep: triggers off, jobs suspended, apps stopped (reverse order)\n#   harness_window.sh window [--adf]   # start -> run -> stop, one shot\n#\n# *--adf create-runs pl_flow_a_customers + pl_flow_a_orders. Flow A loads\n#  SNOWFLAKE — after the Snowflake subscription lapses (v1.1 W1, #588) these runs\n#  will fail; the mockdata jobs (file landing) keep working regardless.\n#  Airflow DAGs are NOT triggered here — they fire on their own cron while the\n#  scheduler is awake (or trigger manually in the Airflow UI).\nset -euo pipefail\n\nSUB=\"37e710fd-b956-4a2e-94f2-b132bb26b3d6\"\nRG=\"dataq-rg\"\nADF=\"dataq-harness-adf\"\nARM=\"https://management.azure.com/subscriptions/$SUB/resourceGroups/$RG/providers\"\nAPP_API=\"2024-03-01\"\nJOB_API=\"2024-08-02-preview\"\nAIRFLOW_URL=\"https://dataq-harness-airflow.purplefield-f7322a1b.westus2.azurecontainerapps.io\"\n\n# Wake order matters: broker first, scheduler before workers. Stop is the reverse.\nAPPS_START=(dataq-harness-redis dataq-harness-airflow dataq-harness-airflow-worker dataq-harness-airflow-trigger)\nAPPS_STOP=(dataq-harness-airflow-trigger dataq-harness-airflow-worker dataq-harness-airflow dataq-harness-redis)\nTRIGGERS=(tr_customers_daily tr_orders_landed)\nJOBS=(mockdata-orders mockdata-inventory mockdata-tracking mockdata-feedback mockdata-supply)\n\nlog() { printf '%s %s\\n' \"$(date +%H:%M:%S)\" \"$*\"; }\n\napp_state() { az containerapp show -n \"$1\" -g \"$RG\" --query properties.runningStatus -o tsv 2>/dev/null; }\n\nwait_app() { # wait_app <name> <wanted-state>\n  local name=$1 want=$2 i\n  for i in $(seq 1 60); do\n    [[ \"$(app_state \"$name\")\" == \"$want\" ]] && { log \"  $name -> $want\"; return 0; }\n    sleep 5\n  done\n  log \"  TIMEOUT: $name never reached $want\"; return 1\n}\n\ncmd_status() {\n  az containerapp list -g \"$RG\" --query \"[?starts_with(name,'dataq-harness')].{name:name, running:properties.runningStatus}\" -o table\n  az datafactory trigger list -g \"$RG\" --factory-name \"$ADF\" --query \"[].{name:name, state:properties.runtimeState}\" -o table\n  local j\n  for j in \"${JOBS[@]}\"; do\n    printf '%s last execution: ' \"$j\"\n    az rest --method GET --url \"$ARM/Microsoft.App/jobs/$j/executions?api-version=$JOB_API\" -o json 2>/dev/null |\n      python3 -c \"import json,sys; v=json.load(sys.stdin)['value']; print(v[0]['properties']['status']+' @ '+v[0]['properties']['startTime'] if v else 'none')\"\n  done\n}\n\ncmd_start() {\n  local app t j\n  log \"Waking harness (billing ~CAD 0.70/hour while awake)...\"\n  for app in \"${APPS_START[@]}\"; do\n    az rest --method POST --url \"$ARM/Microsoft.App/containerApps/$app/start?api-version=$APP_API\" -o none 2>/dev/null || true\n    wait_app \"$app\" Running\n    if [[ \"$app\" == \"dataq-harness-airflow\" ]]; then\n      log \"  waiting for Airflow /health...\"\n      for _ in $(seq 1 60); do\n        curl -sf --max-time 10 \"$AIRFLOW_URL/health\" >/dev/null 2>&1 && { log \"  Airflow healthy\"; break; }\n        sleep 10\n      done\n    fi\n  done\n  for t in \"${TRIGGERS[@]}\"; do\n    az datafactory trigger start -g \"$RG\" --factory-name \"$ADF\" --name \"$t\" >/dev/null && log \"  ADF trigger $t -> Started\"\n  done\n  for j in \"${JOBS[@]}\"; do\n    az rest --method POST --url \"$ARM/Microsoft.App/jobs/$j/resume?api-version=$JOB_API\" -o none 2>/dev/null && log \"  job $j resumed\" || true\n  done\n  log \"Harness is UP. Remember to '$0 stop' when done.\"\n}\n\ncmd_run() {\n  local with_adf=${1:-} j execs=()\n  log \"Starting the 5 mockdata job executions (parallel)...\"\n  for j in \"${JOBS[@]}\"; do\n    local ename\n    ename=$(az rest --method POST --url \"$ARM/Microsoft.App/jobs/$j/start?api-version=$JOB_API\" -o json 2>/dev/null | python3 -c \"import json,sys; print(json.load(sys.stdin).get('name',''))\")\n    log \"  $j -> execution ${ename:-<unknown>}\"\n    execs+=(\"$j:$ename\")\n  done\n  log \"Waiting for job executions to finish (timeout 35m each)...\"\n  local pair status\n  for pair in \"${execs[@]}\"; do\n    j=${pair%%:*}; local e=${pair#*:}\n    [[ -z \"$e\" ]] && continue\n    for _ in $(seq 1 70); do\n      status=$(az rest --method GET --url \"$ARM/Microsoft.App/jobs/$j/executions/$e?api-version=$JOB_API\" -o json 2>/dev/null | python3 -c \"import json,sys; print(json.load(sys.stdin)['properties']['status'])\" 2>/dev/null || echo Unknown)\n      [[ \"$status\" != \"Running\" && \"$status\" != \"Unknown\" ]] && break\n      sleep 30\n    done\n    log \"  $j: $status\"\n  done\n  if [[ \"$with_adf\" == \"--adf\" ]]; then\n    log \"Triggering Flow-A ADF pipelines (needs live Snowflake!)...\"\n    local p rid\n    for p in pl_flow_a_customers pl_flow_a_orders; do\n      rid=$(az datafactory pipeline create-run -g \"$RG\" --factory-name \"$ADF\" --name \"$p\" --query runId -o tsv)\n      log \"  $p -> run $rid\"\n      for _ in $(seq 1 60); do\n        status=$(az datafactory pipeline-run show -g \"$RG\" --factory-name \"$ADF\" --run-id \"$rid\" --query status -o tsv)\n        [[ \"$status\" != \"InProgress\" && \"$status\" != \"Queued\" ]] && break\n        sleep 15\n      done\n      log \"  $p: $status\"\n    done\n  fi\n  log \"Run phase done. Airflow DAGs fire on their own cron while awake (or trigger in the UI: $AIRFLOW_URL).\"\n}\n\ncmd_stop() {\n  local t j app\n  log \"Putting the harness back to sleep...\"\n  for t in \"${TRIGGERS[@]}\"; do\n    az datafactory trigger stop -g \"$RG\" --factory-name \"$ADF\" --name \"$t\" >/dev/null && log \"  ADF trigger $t -> Stopped\"\n  done\n  for j in \"${JOBS[@]}\"; do\n    az rest --method POST --url \"$ARM/Microsoft.App/jobs/$j/suspend?api-version=$JOB_API\" -o none 2>/dev/null && log \"  job $j suspended\" || true\n  done\n  for app in \"${APPS_STOP[@]}\"; do\n    az rest --method POST --url \"$ARM/Microsoft.App/containerApps/$app/stop?api-version=$APP_API\" -o none 2>/dev/null || true\n    wait_app \"$app\" Stopped\n  done\n  log \"Harness is DOWN (burn back to ~CAD 4.5/day, app only).\"\n}\n\ncase \"${1:-}\" in\n  status) cmd_status ;;\n  start)  cmd_start ;;\n  run)    cmd_run \"${2:-}\" ;;\n  stop)   cmd_stop ;;\n  window) cmd_start; cmd_run \"${2:-}\"; cmd_stop ;;\n  *) grep '^#' \"$0\" | sed 's/^# \\{0,1\\}//' | head -20; exit 1 ;;\nesac\n"
}
```
